% --- CHAPTER 33 START ---
\section*{Chapter 33: Speculative decoding: draft + target model with rejection sampling correctness proof}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Chapter 33: Speculative decoding: draft + target model with rejection sampling correctness proof}

\section*{Chapter 33: Speculative Decoding}

\subsection*{1. Motivation}

Autoregressive sampling from a transformer (Chapter~25) is latency-bound: each new token requires one full forward pass over $O(L)$ layers. Even with a KV cache (Chapter~32) eliminating recomputation over the prefix, the next token still costs one sequential pass through the target $\pi$. On modern GPUs this is \emph{memory-bandwidth bound} -- the matmuls underutilize the compute units because we load the entire weight matrix to produce a single token's logits.

Speculative decoding (Leviathan et al.\ 2022; Chen et al.\ 2023) exploits a beautiful observation: a single forward pass of $\pi$ over $K+1$ tokens costs roughly the same wall-clock time as a pass over $1$ token, because the bottleneck is weight-loading, not arithmetic. So if we can \emph{guess} the next $K$ tokens cheaply, we can verify all $K$ in parallel with one pass of $\pi$ -- and provably get samples from $\pi$ exactly.

\subsection*{2. Definitions}

\begin{definition}[Draft model]
A small, fast autoregressive model $\pi_d$ (typically $5$--$10\times$ smaller than the target) generating $K$ candidate tokens $\tilde x_1, \ldots, \tilde x_K$ sequentially.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Target model]
The model $\pi$ whose distribution we actually want to sample from. After the draft proposes $\tilde x_{1:K}$, we run one forward pass of $\pi$ on the prefix concatenated with $\tilde x_{1:K}$, producing $\pi(\cdot \mid x_{<i})$ at every position $i \in \{1,\ldots,K+1\}$ in parallel.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Rejection-sampling acceptance rule]
Process draft tokens left-to-right. Accept $\tilde x_i$ with probability
\[
a_i \;=\; \min\!\Big(1,\; \tfrac{\pi(\tilde x_i \mid x_{<i})}{\pi_d(\tilde x_i \mid x_{<i})}\Big).
\]
On the \emph{first} rejection at position $i$, discard $\tilde x_i, \tilde x_{i+1}, \ldots$ and resample from the residual distribution
\[
r_i(y) \;=\; \frac{\max\!\big(0,\; \pi(y \mid x_{<i}) - \pi_d(y \mid x_{<i})\big)}{Z_i}, \qquad Z_i \;=\; \sum_y \max(0, \pi(y) - \pi_d(y)).
\]
If all $K$ drafts accept, sample one bonus token from the target's free $(K{+}1)^{\text{th}}$ logit.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Wall-clock speedup]
Let $\bar n \in [1, K+1]$ be the mean tokens accepted per round and $\alpha = c_\pi / c_{\pi_d}$ the cost ratio. Then
\[
\text{speedup} \;\approx\; \frac{\bar n}{1 + K/\alpha}.
\]
For $\alpha \gg K$, speedup $\to \bar n$, capped at $K+1$.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{3. Theorems and Proofs}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness: exact equivalence to sampling from $\pi$]\label{thm:correctness}
For any draft distribution $\pi_d$ with $\mathrm{supp}(\pi_d) \supseteq \mathrm{supp}(\pi)$, the speculative-decoding output at any single position is distributed exactly as $\pi$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix a position and condition on $x_{<i}$; write $\pi(y) := \pi(y \mid x_{<i})$ and $\pi_d(y) := \pi_d(y \mid x_{<i})$. The output equals a token $x$ via exactly one of two disjoint events.

\textbf{Case 1 -- accept the draft.} The draft proposes $\tilde x = x$ (probability $\pi_d(x)$) and we accept (probability $\min(1, \pi(x)/\pi_d(x))$):
\[
\mathbb{P}_1(x) \;=\; \pi_d(x) \cdot \min\!\Big(1, \tfrac{\pi(x)}{\pi_d(x)}\Big) \;=\; \min(\pi_d(x), \pi(x)).
\]

\textbf{Case 2 -- reject and resample from $r$.} Total rejection probability:
\[
P_{\text{rej}} \;=\; \sum_{\tilde x} \pi_d(\tilde x)\Big(1 - \min\!\big(1, \tfrac{\pi(\tilde x)}{\pi_d(\tilde x)}\big)\Big) \;=\; \sum_{\tilde x} \max(0, \pi_d(\tilde x) - \pi(\tilde x)).
\]
Since $\sum_y \pi = \sum_y \pi_d = 1$,
\[
\sum_y \max(0, \pi_d(y) - \pi(y)) \;=\; \sum_y \max(0, \pi(y) - \pi_d(y)) \;=\; Z,
\]
so $P_{\text{rej}} = Z$. After rejection we sample from $r$, contributing
\[
\mathbb{P}_2(x) \;=\; Z \cdot r(x) \;=\; \max(0, \pi(x) - \pi_d(x)).
\]

\textbf{Sum.} If $\pi(x) \le \pi_d(x)$: $\mathbb{P}_1 + \mathbb{P}_2 = \pi(x) + 0 = \pi(x)$. If $\pi(x) > \pi_d(x)$: $\mathbb{P}_1 + \mathbb{P}_2 = \pi_d(x) + (\pi(x) - \pi_d(x)) = \pi(x)$. Hence $\mathbb{P}(\text{output}=x) = \pi(x)$ for all $x$. \qedhere
\end{proof}

The argument extends position-by-position: once $\tilde x_i$ is accepted, $x_i$ has the marginal $\pi(\cdot \mid x_{<i})$, and the chain rule gives joint sampling from $\pi$ over the full $K{+}1$ block.

\begin{proposition}[Expected accepted prefix length]\label{prop:expected}
Let $\alpha_j = \mathbb{E}_{\tilde x_j \sim \pi_d}[\min(1, \pi(\tilde x_j)/\pi_d(\tilde x_j))]$ be the marginal acceptance probability at position $j$. The expected number of accepted draft tokens in a $K$-token round is
\[
\bar n_K \;=\; \sum_{i=1}^{K} \prod_{j=1}^{i} \alpha_j.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Sketch]
Let $A_j = \mathbb{1}[\text{first }j\text{ drafts all accepted}]$. Conditional on the prefix, acceptance is independent across $j$, so $\mathbb{E}[A_j] = \prod_{j'\le j}\alpha_{j'}$. Sum by linearity of expectation. \qedhere
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[TV-distance form]
Using $\alpha = \sum_y \min(\pi(y), \pi_d(y)) = 1 - \tfrac{1}{2}\|\pi - \pi_d\|_1$, acceptance is governed by total variation distance.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Optimal draft is the target]\label{cor:optimal}
If $\pi_d = \pi$, then $\alpha_j = 1$ and $\bar n_K = K$ -- but no compute is saved because the draft costs as much as the target. The interesting regime is a cheap draft slightly worse than the target.
\end{corollary}

\subsection*{4. Code Sketch and Benchmarks}

The companion notebook (\texttt{cells.json}) implements \texttt{speculative\_decode(draft, target, prompt, K)} using toy categorical distributions over a small vocabulary -- no GPT inference is required, since the math \emph{is} the chapter (Chapter~32 covers GPT-on-MPS benchmarking).

\begin{example}[Experiments]
(i) Draw $10{,}000$ tokens via the speculative protocol and compare against direct samples from $\pi$; a $\chi^2$ goodness-of-fit test fails to reject. (ii) Sweep noise $q$ where $\pi_d = (1-q)\pi + q\,\mathrm{Unif}$; plot $\bar n$ and predicted wall-clock speedup at $K=4,\ \alpha=7$. (iii) Identify the breakeven $q$ at which speculative decoding loses to plain decoding.
\end{example}

\subsection*{5. Connection to LLMs}

Production inference engines -- \textbf{vLLM}, \textbf{llama.cpp}, \textbf{MLX-lm}, \textbf{TensorRT-LLM} -- all ship speculative decoding. Reported wall-clock speedups: $1.5$--$3\times$ for code/translation (high draft-target agreement) and $1.1$--$1.5\times$ for open-ended chat. Variants:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Self-speculation / Medusa heads.} Attach extra prediction heads to the target itself, eliminating the separate draft model.
  \item \textbf{Tree attention / SpecInfer.} Verify multiple candidate continuations in one forward pass via causal-mask trees, raising $\bar n$.
  \item \textbf{EAGLE / Lookahead.} Train a tiny draft head conditioned on the target's hidden state for higher acceptance.
\end{itemize}

Theorem~\ref{thm:correctness} transfers verbatim to all these schemes: as long as verification uses the rejection rule with the residual-distribution fallback, the output marginal is \emph{exactly} $\pi$. No quality is sacrificed for the speedup -- a rare free lunch in deep learning.

% --- CHAPTER 34 START ---
\section*{Chapter 34: Quantization: int8 and int4 post-training quantization with measured perplexity / throughput tradeoffs}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Chapter 34: Quantization: int8 and int4 post-training quantization with measured perplexity / throughput tradeoffs}

\subsection*{Motivation}

Llama-3 70B in \texttt{bfloat16} is 140~GB of weights -- larger than any consumer GPU's VRAM and most workstation RAM. The same model in \texttt{int4} is roughly 40~GB and fits on a single 48~GB card with room for the KV-cache. The accuracy cost? For well-calibrated \texttt{int8} quantization, perplexity typically rises by less than $0.5\%$; for \texttt{int4}, by $1$--$3\%$. Quantization is the single technique most responsible for moving frontier-class models out of the data center and onto laptops.

We restrict attention to \emph{post-training quantization} (PTQ): take an already-trained fp16/bf16 checkpoint and replace its weights with low-precision approximations without further gradient updates. The mathematical object of study is a map $Q : \mathbb{R}^{m\times n}\to\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}$ together with a dequantizer $D$ that approximates the identity, $D(Q(W))\approx W$. Two questions follow: (i) what is the worst-case reconstruction error $\|W - D(Q(W))\|$ (Ch.~6 norm theory), and (ii) how does that error propagate to the cross-entropy and hence the perplexity $e^{\mathrm{CE}}$ (Ch.~17)?

\subsection*{Definitions}

\begin{definition}[Affine quantizer]
Fix bit-width $b\in\{8,4,2\}$ and let $N=2^b$. An \emph{affine} quantizer with scale $s>0$ and zero-point $z\in\mathbb{Z}$ is the function
\[
Q_{s,z}(w) \;=\; \mathrm{clip}\!\big(\mathrm{round}(w/s) - z,\ q_{\min},\ q_{\max}\big),
\]
where the integer range is $[q_{\min},q_{\max}]=[-N/2,\,N/2-1]$ (signed) or $[0,\,N-1]$ (unsigned). The dequantizer is $D_{s,z}(q)=(q+z)\cdot s$. The map is \emph{symmetric} when $z=0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Calibration]
Given a calibration set $\{x_1,\dots,x_K\}\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ and a layer with weight $W$, the scale $s$ is chosen to cover the dynamic range observed during a forward pass -- typically $s=\max_{ij}|w_{ij}|/(N/2-1)$ (max-abs) or the $99.99$-percentile absolute value (percentile clipping, robust to outliers).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Granularity]
\emph{Per-tensor} quantization uses one scalar $s$ for the entire matrix. \emph{Per-row} (per-output-channel) gives each row $W_{i,:}$ its own $s_i$. \emph{Per-channel-group} breaks each row into groups of $128$ contiguous elements, each with its own scale. The metadata cost in bits per weight is, respectively, $O(1/mn)$, $O(1/n)$, $O(1/g)$, traded against tighter dynamic range.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Weight-only quantization, W$b$A16]
Only the weight tensors are stored in \texttt{int}$b$; activations stay in fp16/bf16. The matmul dequantizes weights on-the-fly. This avoids the catastrophic effect of \emph{activation outliers} identified by Dettmers et al.\ (2022, LLM.int8()).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Layer-local objective]
For a linear layer $y=Wx$ and calibration matrix $X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times K}$, the \emph{layer-local quantization problem} is
\[
\widehat W \;\in\; \arg\min_{\widehat W \in \mathcal{Q}}\; \big\|WX - \widehat W X\big\|_F^2,
\]
where $\mathcal{Q}$ is the discrete set of representable quantized matrices. This is the objective minimized by GPTQ (Frantar et al., 2023).
\end{definition}

\subsection*{Theorems}

\begin{theorem}[Round-to-nearest error bound]\label{thm:rtn}
Let $Q_s$ be the symmetric affine quantizer with scale $s$ and let $\widehat w = D_s(Q_s(w)) = s\cdot\mathrm{round}(w/s)$. For every $w$ in the representable range,
\[
|\,w - \widehat w\,| \;\le\; s/2.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By definition of round-to-nearest, for any real $u$, $|u - \mathrm{round}(u)|\le 1/2$. Set $u=w/s$: $|w/s - \mathrm{round}(w/s)|\le 1/2$. Multiply by $s>0$ to get $|w - s\cdot\mathrm{round}(w/s)| = |w - \widehat w|\le s/2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Frobenius bound]\label{cor:frob}
For an $m\times n$ matrix $W$ quantized symmetrically per-tensor with scale $s$, $\|W-\widehat W\|_F \le (s/2)\sqrt{mn}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{theorem}[Per-row beats per-tensor on heterogeneous matrices]\label{thm:perrow}
Let $W\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}$ have rows with $\ell_\infty$ norms $r_i = \|W_{i,:}\|_\infty$. With $b$ bits and $N=2^b$:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Per-tensor} scale $s_\star = (\max_i r_i)/(N/2-1)$ gives entry error $\le s_\star/2$, so
\[
\|W-\widehat W\|_F^2 \;\le\; \frac{mn}{4}\cdot\frac{(\max_i r_i)^2}{(N/2-1)^2}.
\]
\item \emph{Per-row} scale $s_i = r_i/(N/2-1)$ gives
\[
\|W-\widehat W\|_F^2 \;\le\; \frac{n}{4(N/2-1)^2}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^m r_i^2.
\]
\end{itemize}
The ratio is
\[
\frac{\text{per-row bound}}{\text{per-tensor bound}} \;=\; \frac{1}{m}\cdot\frac{\sum_i r_i^2}{(\max_i r_i)^2} \;\le\; 1,
\]
with equality iff every row has the same $\ell_\infty$ norm. The gap grows with the variance of $\{r_i\}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Both inequalities follow from Theorem~\ref{thm:rtn} applied entrywise; the ratio identity is algebra. The bound is tight when one row dominates ($\max\gg$ mean), wasting most of the integer grid on small rows.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[GPTQ row decomposition]\label{thm:gptq}
For a single layer with weight $W\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}$ and calibration Hessian $H = XX^\top \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$ (PSD by construction), the layer-local objective decouples row-wise:
\[
\big\|WX-\widehat W X\big\|_F^2 \;=\; \sum_{i=1}^m (W_{i,:} - \widehat W_{i,:})\, H\, (W_{i,:} - \widehat W_{i,:})^\top.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$\|WX - \widehat W X\|_F^2 = \mathrm{tr}\big((W-\widehat W)XX^\top(W-\widehat W)^\top\big) = \sum_i \mathrm{row}_i(W-\widehat W)\,H\,\mathrm{row}_i(W-\widehat W)^\top$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[GPTQ algorithm sketch]
Process columns $j=1,\dots,n$ in order. At step $j$, for each row $i$ quantize $w_{ij}$ to $\widehat w_{ij}$, compute $\delta = w_{ij}-\widehat w_{ij}$, and update unquantized columns $k>j$ by the Hessian-correcting term
\[
w_{ik} \;\leftarrow\; w_{ik} \;-\; \delta\cdot \frac{(H^{-1})_{jk}}{(H^{-1})_{jj}}.
\]
The Cholesky factor of $H^{-1}$ allows this in $O(n^3)$ per layer. The correction absorbs the rounding error of column $j$ into the not-yet-quantized columns, partially cancelling subsequent rounding. The naive RTN baseline is the special case where the correction is omitted.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Code sketch and benchmarks}

The accompanying notebook contains six experiments. (1) A bytes-math table for GPT-2 small (124M), Llama-7B, Llama-70B at fp16/int8/int4. (2) A round-to-nearest implementation \verb|quantize(w, bits)| with measured Frobenius error on a Gaussian matrix. (3) A $64\times 128$ matrix with row norms $r_i = 10^{i/64}$ comparing per-tensor and per-row scales at int4: per-row reduces error by an order of magnitude. (4) A GPTQ implementation on a $d_{\mathrm{in}}=16, d_{\mathrm{out}}=8$ linear layer with 100-sample calibration: GPTQ beats RTN on the layer-output reconstruction error. (5) End-to-end perplexity: a miniature Ch.~27-style numpy GPT is trained briefly, then quantized to int8 and int4 by RTN; perplexity on the training corpus is reported before and after.

\subsection*{Connection to LLMs}

Quantization closes the \emph{deployment gap}. Training requires fp32 master weights (Ch.~14 AdamW state, Ch.~27 mixed precision); inference needs only the forward pass, where weight precision dominates memory bandwidth on modern GPUs. The empirical rule -- $<0.5\%$ perplexity loss for int8, $1$--$3\%$ for int4 with GPTQ -- means a Llama-3 70B user trades roughly $2\%$ perplexity for a $3.5\times$ memory reduction and $2\times$ inference throughput. AWQ, SmoothQuant, and HQQ extend this picture: all minimize variants of Theorem~\ref{thm:gptq}'s layer-local objective, differing in how they reweight by activation statistics. The thread that ties them all to first principles is Theorem~\ref{thm:rtn}: every byte saved costs at most $s/2$ of error per weight, and the entire research program is about choosing $s$ wisely.
